# BCF techniques in the master plan, and the next whitepaper draft

Date of this note: 2026-09-30. This is task 1 of 2. The math and the prior-research survey are in [04-fault-interference-math-and-prior-work.md](04-fault-interference-math-and-prior-work.md).

The roadmap and the six-sprint plan stay as written in `01-roadmap-checklist-milestones.md` and `02-six-sprint-master-plan.md`. This note attaches techniques to those sprints. It does not replace the calendar, the score gates, or the chosen brain: one root coder, one read-only localizer, four stdlib skills, an early first edit, and an explicit `submit_patch`.

## 1. What was read

Competition rules and harness behavior come from the local snapshots in `input/kagglecomp/docs/markdown` (overview, data, getting started, rules; pages saved 2026-09-29) and from `HARNESS_README.md` plus the sample submission inside the data zip. Those sources fix the constraints the techniques have to live inside:

- One model, `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`, for every agent.
- Score is the fraction of hidden tasks whose patch applies and whose pytest run is clean, including the required fail-to-pass and pass-to-pass nodes.
- About 120 hidden tasks share one 12-hour job. A conservative per-task budget is about 4 to 6 minutes.
- `search_similar_code` matches node keys and suffixes, then ranks those nodes by cosine. A free-text query is the wrong shape. The getting-started log records that the query "Server Sent Events" returned 0 nodes.
- The agent never sees `test_patch` or the gold patch. Hints on all 129 train tasks are empty.
- Scratch files under `/workspace` enter the diff. Probes belong in `/tmp`.
- Paper track: deadline 2026-11-12 23:59 UTC, a $35,000 pool, four topic areas (tuning and optimization including PEFT and RL, code comprehension, tasks and benchmarks, graph reasoning). The overview snapshot does not state a per-topic prize split or a scoring rubric. Those stay unstated in the draft.

The Batonic Coding Framework material is `bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md` (compiled 2026-09-28) and `bcf/BCF-contestsubmission_dossier_20260928.html`. The compendium labels its own sections RECOVERED, EXTERNAL, RECONSTRUCTED, or GAP. The original SRS, the v3 graph edition, and several named source notes were not readable when that compendium was compiled. Reconstructed sections are proposals. They enter the lab as hypotheses with a test, and they enter the paper only after a receipt exists.

The dossier is a conversation export with two whitepaper drafts and a provenance ledger. Draft 1 reports turn-count and accuracy figures the dossier itself later withdraws. Draft 2 replaces that results table with a measurement protocol and labels its case studies as illustrations. One illustration, `httpx_6821`, is an invented task. The dossier says so. The train set contains one httpx task, `httpx_3672`, which is a single-file rename and return-type change. This note uses the invented scenario as a pattern name only. Document 04 writes the math. Neither document treats the withdrawn percentages as measurements.

## 2. How a technique earns a place

A technique from the compendium is useful here when it can be checked by code or by pytest, when it fits a 4-to-6-minute task, and when it can lose a fair comparison against the brain already specified. The adoption order follows the compendium's own caution and the sprint calendar:

1. A ledger, so later numbers have receipts.
2. A short spec card and executable gates.
3. Phase scoping on the localizer that the plan already builds in sprint 2.
4. Class-conditional search, including the crash-class predecessor search, as a sprint 3 ablation.
5. Handoff and rescue ablations after the prompt freezes.
6. Packaging, queues, and hosted tracing last, and mostly off the submission path.

## 3. Techniques to incorporate, and the test for each

### 3.1 Ledger and evidence tags

The compendium's ledger is a JSONL file of decisions, with a receipt for every number that might reach the paper. Tags are M for a quantity measured on a named run, D for a quantity derived from measurements, I for an illustration, and H for a hypothesis. Draft 1 is what happens when those tags are missing.

Incorporate it on day 0, before the first harness run, as `lab/ledger.jsonl` next to the plan's `SUBMISSIONS.md`. Every upload row already required by the checklist (date, tag, canary, requests, public score, one-sentence hypothesis) grows a receipt id. Illustrations such as the constructed redirect example are stored with tag I and are rejected by the paper build if a sentence cites them as a score.

Test, at the sprint 6 paper assembly and again on paper day (M8, 2026-11-12): a small checker walks the PDF's source markdown, extracts quantities, and fails the build when a quantity has no receipt id or when the receipt's tag is I or H. The checker is dev-only. It is not a competition skill.

### 3.2 The four tenets, restated so they can fail a test

| Tenet | Operational form in this harness | Where | Test |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Preserve working code | A patch that breaks a neighboring test is reverted before submit | Sprint 3, inside `patch_hygiene` | Inject a one-line regression on 5 dev tasks. The submitted diff lacks that line. F6 on the dev histogram falls across the next full dev run |
| Minimum path | The spec names a `fix_shape` and a `depends_on` list, and the first edit follows that order | Sprint 2 schema, scored in sprint 3 | On resolved dev tasks, median files touched and median changed lines stay in the neighborhood of the gold median (1 file, about 12 lines). On tasks later labeled as causally chained, the first edit lands in a predecessor file |
| Breadcrumbs | The spec card is one JSON object in the trace ledger. Any rescue journal is a file under `/tmp` | Sprint 2 for the card, sprint 5 if rescue is ablated | Every submit row in the ledger contains a spec id. A journal placed under `/workspace` fails the F11 check |
| Machine-checkable done | Gates are Python, schema checks, `git apply --check`, and pytest. The model's opinion that it is finished does not open the gate | Sprint 2 and sprint 3 | A spec that fails the schema is followed by no `edit_file`. A verifier prompt that says the patch looks right cannot by itself call `submit_patch` |

The compendium's stronger wording, that a model score of its own work is structurally impossible, overclaims. The published result to cite is narrower: intrinsic self-correction is unreliable (Huang et al., "Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet", ICLR 2024), and LLM judges prefer their own generations (Panickssery, Bowman, and Feng, "LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations", NeurIPS 2024). The harness consequence is the same. Approval has to be an external check.

### 3.3 A spec card that finishes before the first edit

The compendium's specification phase forbids file access until a spec exists, then localizes, patches, and validates. Under a five-minute cap a long specification competes with the edit. The form that fits this harness is a card, emitted as one JSON object, with:

- `target_symbol`, a name harvested from the issue text
- `fix_shape`, one of `delete`, `replace`, `guard_add`, `check_add`, `iterator_or_logic_rewrite`, `new_code`, `other`
- `depends_on`, a list of symbols that must be fixed earlier, possibly empty
- `acceptance`, the observable the patch should change
- `scope`, files the edit may touch
- `circuit_breaker`, the condition that stops rescue and forces submit of the current tree

The hidden test patch is not an input to this card. The dossier sketched a classifier that read `test_patch`, then withdrew that sketch for the right reason: at score time the agent never receives it.

Incorporate the card on Thursday–Friday of sprint 2 (2026-10-08 and 2026-10-09), which is already the localizer-schema window, milestone M3. The coder prompt requires the card before `edit_file`. The localizer remains read-only, which Saturday 2026-10-10 already audits.

Test, on a 10-task dev slice, two arms at temperature 0:

- Card arm: schema-valid JSON before the first edit.
- Control: the current localize-then-edit prompt with no card.

Keep the card when the slice resolves at least as many tasks and the first edit still lands by tool call 4 on issues shorter than 500 characters. That second clause protects the early-edit rule already in the plan. If the card arm burns the budget before editing, shrink the card again. A lost slice stays in the ledger as a negative result the paper can report.

A dev-only check, Promptfoo or a plain JSON Schema validator against the local vLLM, watches schema breakage across prompt edits. It never ships in `submission.zip`, and it is pointed at the local server so task text stays off hosted endpoints.

### 3.4 Gates that code can refuse

| Gate | Check | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| G0 | Spec JSON matches the schema | Block the first edit |
| G1 | Every symbol in the card was harvested from the issue or returned by a tool | Block the first edit |
| G2 | `git apply --check` accepts the diff | Block submit, one smaller retry |
| G3 | Changed `.py` files parse | Block submit, one smaller retry |
| G4 | The targeted pytest node, when one was named, is the one that runs | Block a wide pytest. This is the existing F10 response |
| G5 | A neighboring test that passed before the edit fails after it | Revert immediately. This is the preserve-working-code tenet |
| G6 | The diff is non-empty at budget exhaustion, unless every candidate failed G5 | Submit the best tree that passed G0–G3 and G5. The harness scores the dirty tree on timeout, which is why the plan forces an early edit |

G5 and G6 land inside `patch_hygiene` on Thursday 2026-10-15 (sprint 3, toward M4). The plan already spends that day killing F11. The extra test is the injected regression in section 3.2, plus one killed run whose tree is dirty: the session must still submit a patch. An empty tree remains F1, and the response remains "edit earlier," which sprint 1 and sprint 2 already own.

Agent-written reproduction scripts, and a separate verifier prompt, may trigger another repair attempt. They may not approve a submit. Approval stays with G2, G3, and the targeted pytest. This is the compendium's V0–V2 versus V3 split, trimmed to the clock: blocking checks are mechanical, and model-graded checks can only spend rescue budget.

### 3.5 Symbol-shaped graph queries

Draft 2 of the whitepaper describes dual-query semantic search over the released embeddings as if a natural-language sentence were a valid query. The harness contract is narrower. `search_similar_code` resolves node keys and suffixes, then applies cosine among the nodes it found. The useful BCF fragment is the one that already matches the plan: harvest symbols from the issue, then query the graph with those symbols. `get_code_neighbors` is the tool that walks callers and callees.

This is sprint 2's existing theme, and M3 already requires F9 (an English query that returns 0 nodes) under 10% of dev failures. The incorporation is a correction of the paper, plus one new arm for crashes, described in document 04: from a crash frame, call `get_code_neighbors` with `called_by` and depth 2, and compare gold-file hits against patching the frame itself. Sunday 2026-10-11, the symbol-oracle versus blind comparison, is the right day to record the first hit rates.

Repo-specific keyword lists overfit the four public training repositories and will not transfer to the private test repositories. They stay out of the prompt. The plan's F12 rule already deletes FastAPI and Rich layout assumptions when they show up on `requests`.

### 3.6 Class-conditional prompts, with a small K

The dossier's class-aware extension is the right idea and the wrong implementation if it grows a large taxonomy of exception types or if it reads the test patch. Document 04 shows why adding classes is not automatically faster. The experiment that belongs in the plan is small and pre-registered:

- K = 1. No class. The current localizer.
- K = 3. `crash`, `logic_or_contract`, `feature`, and `unknown`. Unknown uses the K = 1 search.
- K = 4. Split `logic_or_contract` into `logic` and `edge_case`.

Input is the issue text alone. The run is one controlled day in sprint 3, on the dev split, at temperature 0. It graduates under the rule the plan already uses: a gain of two or more dev tasks and no drop on the `requests` holdout. Otherwise the paper reports the measured coverage and the measured search cost, and the submission keeps K = 1 or whichever smaller arm won. Canary is scored at the M4 boundary, not after this tweak in isolation.

### 3.7 Fix order for chained faults

Thirty-eight of the 129 gold patches touch two or more files. Multi-file is a location count. It is a different predicate from "two faults, one of which hides the other." The taxonomy in section 3.9 separates them. Where the gold patch and the issue support a causal order, the spec card's `depends_on` list is a partial order, and the first edit is a source of that order.

The test waits until the taxonomy has marked a handful of dev tasks as causally chained (tier 4 in section 3.9). The metric is `root_cause_order_error`: the first edit touches a successor while a predecessor gold file is still untouched. The ablation is an ordered card against a card whose `depends_on` is ignored. This is a sprint 3 measurement on the way to M4. It does not block M3.

### 3.8 Handoff packet, as an ablation

The compendium compares a full transcript with a reduced handoff packet. The literature it points at is mixed: read-heavy multi-agent work can spend many times the tokens of a single agent, and write-heavy coding work has been reported to need the full trace. The plan's `AgentTool` with `skip_summarization: true` is already a packet. It has not been compared with the full localizer transcript.

The comparison runs only if Wednesday 2026-10-14 is free of the sequential-agent A/B the plan already scheduled. Three arms, 10 dev tasks, same cap:

- T0, full localizer transcript returned to the root.
- T1, the packet produced by `skip_summarization`.
- T2, the packet plus the last tool result verbatim.

The winner is the arm with more resolves. A tie keeps T1, because it is the plan's default. Token counts are recorded so the paper can say what the packet saved. The compendium's 120k-token handoff budget conflicts with the grader's 32,768-token context and is not used.

### 3.9 The 129-task taxonomy, as a paper resource

Draft 2's durable idea is a human-verified taxonomy of the training tasks, offered as a resource under the tasks-and-benchmarks topic. The labeling schema:

| Field | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| `instance_id` | Copied from `tasks.jsonl` |
| `repo` | `fastapi`, `rich`, `requests`, or `httpx` |
| `task_type` | `bug_fix`, `feature`, or `compat_or_refactor` |
| `bug_category` | One mechanism code, or `other`. The codebook starts open and is frozen before scoring |
| `complexity_tier` | 1, one file, one hunk, at most 5 lines. 2, one file, larger. 3, two or more files, independent. 4, two or more files, causal. 5, cross-module new behavior |
| `multi_bug` | True when two defects are causally dependent. A multi-file gold patch with independent hunks stays false |
| `root_cause_file` | From the gold patch, offline |
| `root_cause_symbol` | Must exist as a graph node when `symbol_in_graph` is true |
| `fix_shape_type` | Same enum as the spec card |
| `rationale` | One sentence |
| `human_override` | True when a human replaced a model suggestion |
| `label_confidence` | `high` or `low` |

ProArt owns this in sprint 2 (2026-10-08 to 2026-10-14), overlapping M3. The codebook is open during the first pass and frozen before any score is stratified by label. Held-out tasks (`requests` and `httpx`, 14 tasks) are labeled after the brain freezes, so label language cannot leak into prompts. A hosted model is not sent task text while the data-use question is open. A local Gemma pass may propose labels. `human_override` records every change a person makes. Labels of the gold root are an offline research artifact. They never enter a submission prompt, a skill, or a training mixture.

The first scientific use is the class-coverage experiment in document 04. The second is to find one real tier-4 train task that can replace the invented `httpx_6821` story as a worked example, still labeled as a training-set case study.

### 3.10 Rescue, after the prompt freezes

The compendium's rescue machine goes from normal operation to intake, then to a minimum baseline, then to an attempted induction, with three strikes and an immediate revert on regression. It is a real idea and a dangerous default under this clock, because a rescue loop can spend the entire task re-deriving context.

It runs as an ablation in sprint 5 (2026-10-29 to 2026-11-04, M6), after the M4 prompt freeze, and only on dev tasks whose failure code is F4 (right file, behavior still wrong) or F6 (a pass-to-pass test broke). The journal lives in `/tmp`. Metrics are `rescue_trigger_rate` and `rescue_recovery_rate` (triggered tasks that then resolve). Rescue cannot set the submit bit. If recovery is below the cost in extra tool calls, the paper reports the negative and the submission omits the loop.

### 3.11 What the early-edit rule already is

The compendium's empty-patch discussion and the harness timeout behavior are the same fact: a session that ends with a dirty tree can still score, and a session that ends with a clean tree scores zero on that task. The plan's early first edit is the implementation. No new mechanism is required. The ledger records F1 before and after sprint 2 so the paper can treat the drop, if it happens, as a measured efficiency result.

## 4. What stays out of the submission

| Item | Reason it stays out |
| --- | --- |
| Langfuse, LiteLLM, or any hosted trace of task text | Task text leaving the machine is a data-rules question. A local JSONL ledger covers the need |
| The sample rank-4 LoRA | The plan already requires a two-task canary gain before any adapter ships |
| A multi-agent tree as the default | Sprint 2 already A/B tests a sequential agent on 10 tasks if the root wanders. The default remains one root until that test wins on resolves at the same cap |
| A skill or classifier that reads `test_patch` | The agent does not receive it at score time |
| Repository-specific keyword maps | They fit four public repos and the test repos are private |
| A hosted labeling model inside the sandbox | The sandbox has no network, and only Gemma may be called |
| SWE-bench Verified as a headline number | Different harness, overlapping `requests`, and the compendium already demotes it. This paper's numbers come from this train set and this harness |
| The compendium's 120k-token budgets | The grader context is 32,768 tokens |
| Draft 1's turn counts and accuracy percentages | The dossier withdraws them |
| Reconstructed SRS prose presented as the original specification | The compendium marks those sections reconstructed |

## 5. Next whitepaper draft

Working title: "Class-conditional localization under fault masking, for a budgeted Gemma coding agent."

The title names the claim document 04 actually supports. A fallback title if the class ablation is flat is "What a five-minute coding agent can check: specification gates, symbol queries, and a ceiling ladder on the Gemma 4 developer benchmark." Both titles can be written from the same outline. The data pick the title on paper day.

### 5.1 What this draft changes relative to draft 2

- The results table stays a protocol until receipts exist. Withdrawn draft-1 figures do not return.
- The graph section describes key-and-suffix lookup followed by cosine. Free-text semantic search is described as a query shape that returns empty, with the getting-started log as the receipt.
- The classifier section states that the input is the issue text. It states that `test_patch` is unavailable at score time.
- A formal cost model, written in document 04, replaces the sentence that effectiveness simply tracks the number and fidelity of exception classes.
- `httpx_6821` remains available as a constructed illustration of reachability masking, with the invention disclosed in the same paragraph. Turn counts from that illustration are omitted. A real tier-4 training task replaces it if the taxonomy finds one before 2026-11-12.
- Every quantity carries a receipt id and a tag M, D, I, or H.

### 5.2 Outline, and the host topic each part serves

The overview names four topic areas and one $35,000 pool. This draft aims at a coherent paper that uses all four, with one primary contribution so a reader can see what was measured.

| Section | Primary topic it serves | What is in it by paper day |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The clock and the early edit | Tuning and optimization | The 12-hour budget model, the speed ratio between the 5090 and the grader, F1 before and after the early-edit rule. PEFT appears only if an adapter beat the frozen prompt by two canary tasks. RL stays a reported experiment unless it won that same comparison |
| Symbol queries and the caller cone | Code comprehension and graph reasoning | The key-match contract, F9, and the crash-frame versus `called_by` hit rates |
| The coverage threshold | Code comprehension | The formula and the inequality in document 04, plus the K = 1, K = 3, and K = 4 measurements if sprint 3 has run |
| A taxonomy of 129 training tasks | Tasks and benchmarks | The schema, the frozen codebook, inter-label notes, and the count of tier-4 and `multi_bug` items. This is a resource the host invited |
| Ceiling ladder | Tasks and benchmarks | Gold replay, file oracle, symbol oracle, and blind, on the frozen canary. The fork between localization work and literal-repair work is a method result even when public scores are still low |
| Threats and withdrawn claims | All four | Single-fault assumption, issue text as a weak class signal, constructed examples, and an explicit list of figures that draft 1 contained and this draft omits |

### 5.3 Ideas worth writing, ranked by how close they are to a receipt

1. The coverage threshold. Independent faults justify a class only when causal coverage exceeds the subset's share of the search space. Masking makes the observed exception a bad proxy for that coverage. This is the new formal content, and it is writable before any new leaderboard point. Document 04 is the draft text.
2. The ceiling ladder as a diagnostic that other entrants can rerun on the public train set. Gold replay near 20/20 means the harness is faithful. The gap from the file oracle down to the blind agent is the localization gap. The gap from gold replay down to the file oracle is the editing gap. Sprint 1 is scheduled to measure both by 2026-10-03 (M1).
3. Spec-card validity against resolution. Draft 2 suggested scoring spec quality. The measurable version is narrower: among dev tasks, resolution rate given a schema-valid card versus resolution rate without one, at the same tool budget. A positive association is a mechanism result. A flat association is also a result, and it would mean the card is documentation rather than a cause.
4. The taxonomy as the artifact other people can use after the competition. Human overrides are part of the release notes for that artifact. Stratified scores by tier are interesting only after the codebook is frozen.
5. Non-results, written on purpose. The constructed turn counts, the free-text embedding claim, the classifier that wanted `test_patch`, and any class count K that lost the ablation. A short non-results section is how this draft stays credible next to draft 1.
6. One real chained training task, narrated with the residual-failure rule from document 04, once the taxonomy marks a tier-4 gold patch. Until that label exists, the paper uses the constructed redirect pattern and says it is constructed.

### 5.4 Calendar for the writing

The plan already spends sprint 6 (2026-11-05 to 2026-11-11) assembling the PDF and paper day (Thursday 2026-11-12, 23:59 UTC) submitting it or writing a one-paragraph skip into `GATES.md`. The drafting order that matches the evidence is:

- Week of sprint 2: taxonomy codebook and the first 30 labels, on ProArt, while the PowerSpec does localization.
- Week of sprint 3: write the method section and run the K ablation. The method section can be drafted from document 04 before the numbers exist, with empty receipt slots.
- Week of sprint 4: write the adapter section as either a win with a receipt or a decision to keep the base model.
- Sprint 6: fill receipts, run the quantity checker, choose the title from the data, submit on the 12th.

Prediction entries remain the priority when the two calendars conflict on 2026-11-11, as the master plan already says.

# Fault masking, exception classes, and the math for the next whitepaper draft

Date of this note: 2026-09-30. This is task 2 of 2. How the techniques attach to the sprint plan is in [03-bcf-techniques-into-the-plan.md](03-bcf-techniques-into-the-plan.md).

The statement under study is:

> I aim to build BCF to help solve more issues sooner by classifying errors into exception classes. This effectively limits the search space to the most probable root causes. [Flesh out how BCF can solve issues due to fault interference, fault masking, and multi-step chained issues such as the `httpx_6821` example.] Therefore, the speed and overall effectiveness of the algorithm will simply depend on the adjustments to the number and fidelity of the exception categories.

The first two sentences describe a real and useful phenomenon. The last sentence names the right two knobs and then flattens a tradeoff into a monotone. Fault masking and fault interference are further phenomena, and they are why "fidelity" has to be split in two before the knobs are turned. This note names each phenomenon, writes the cost model, gives prose that can go into the next draft, and then, in its own section, surveys the prior research.

No figure in this note is a competition score. The only httpx task in the published training set is `httpx_3672`. The identifier `httpx_6821` is a constructed example from the dossier, which says it was invented.

## 1. The phenomena in the statement

Five separable ideas are packed into the paragraph.

| # | Name | What the statement is reaching for | Where it is worked out |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Restricted search | A class retrieves a subset of candidate roots, and the agent searches that subset first | Section 2 |
| 2 | Coverage threshold | The subset is faster than the full set only when causal coverage exceeds the subset's share of the universe | Section 2 |
| 3 | Two fidelities | Naming the observed exception accurately is a different event from having the true root inside the subset | Section 3 |
| 4 | Fault masking | One fault changes whether another fault executes, infects state, or propagates, so the observed failure is not the union of the single-fault failures | Section 4 |
| 5 | Fault interference, and chained repair | When both faults are visible, their failing tests distort each other's ranking. When one hides the other, the repair order is a partial order, and a newly appeared failure can be unmasking | Sections 5 and 6 |

Phenomenon 1 is standard in model-based diagnosis: a hypothesis set is useful when it contains the fault (de Kleer and Williams, "Diagnosing Multiple Faults", Artificial Intelligence, 1987; Reiter, "A Theory of Diagnosis from First Principles", Artificial Intelligence, 1987). Phenomenon 2 is the calculation that tells you when the hypothesis set is worth using. Phenomena 4 and 5 are the software-testing results of Debroy and Wong (ISSRE 2009) and of DiGiuseppe and Jones (Empirical Software Engineering, 2015). The statement treats 4 and 5 as more classes. They are different search operators.

## 2. Restricted search and the coverage threshold

Let S be the set of candidate root locations the agent might have to inspect, with `|S| = n`. A location is a file or a symbol, whichever grain the localizer actually retrieves. The agent inspects locations one at a time and stops when it inspects the true root R. Inspection order inside a set is treated as uniform. That matches a localizer which returns an unordered shortlist more closely than it matches an oracle ranking.

A classifier, given the issue text x, returns a class c(x) and a subset A ⊂ S with `|A| = m`. Causal coverage is

```text
α = P(R ∈ A)
```

On a hit, the expected number of inspections is `(m + 1) / 2`. On a miss, the agent inspects all m members of A and then searches the remainder, which costs

```text
m + (n - m + 1) / 2
```

The law of total expectation gives

```text
C(m, α) = α · (m + 1) / 2  +  (1 - α) · [ m + (n - m + 1) / 2 ]
```

which simplifies to

```text
C(m, α) = (m + 1) / 2  +  (1 - α) · n / 2
```

The first term is the cost of searching the subset. The second term is the penalty for missing, charged as half the universe because the fallback search is itself a uniform search over what remains, rearranged. Exhaustive search is the special case `m = n`, `α = 1`, and costs `(n + 1) / 2`.

Set the subset cost strictly below the exhaustive cost. The algebra reduces to a threshold:

```text
α > m / n
```

A class earns its keep when the probability that the true root sits inside the retrieved subset is greater than the subset's share of the candidate universe. For a universe of 40 files and a class that retrieves 8, coverage has to exceed 0.20. At exactly 0.20 the class ties exhaustive search. Below 0.20 the class is slower than reading the whole universe, because every miss still pays for the 8.

| Search policy | Subset size m | Causal coverage α | Expected inspections, n = 40 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Exhaustive | 40 | 1.00 | 20.5 |
| Tight class, root always inside | 8 | 1.00 | 4.5 |
| Tight class, coverage 0.60 | 8 | 0.60 | 12.5 |
| Tight class, at the threshold | 8 | 0.20 | 20.5 |
| Tight class, root always outside | 8 | 0.00 | 24.5 |

Number of classes K enters because both m and α depend on K. A finer partition tends to shrink m. It also tends to lower α, because the classifier has more ways to be wrong and because a tighter bucket is easier to miss the root with. Those two movements pull C in opposite directions, so C as a function of K is U-shaped whenever α falls as m falls. There is a K-star that minimizes expected inspections. Past K-star, another exception category makes the agent slower.

This is the same shape as a bias-variance tradeoff on a quantizer of the root-cause space. The information the class carries about the root is I(c; R). The cost of acting on a wrong class is the miss penalty `(1 - α) · n / 2`. Fidelity and K are the arguments of C. They trade off. They do not each help on their own.

The uniform-inspection model is the baseline the ablation should beat. If the localizer ranks inside A, replace `(m + 1) / 2` with the expected rank of R inside A, and replace the miss term accordingly. The threshold inequality changes its constants and keeps its shape: a smaller set helps only while the probability of containing the root stays high enough to pay for the misses.

## 3. Observational fidelity and causal coverage

The word "fidelity" in the statement covers two events.

- Observational fidelity β is the probability that the predicted class matches the class of the exception the issue text describes. An issue that says `AssertionError` and is labeled `crash` scores well on β.
- Causal coverage α is the probability that the true root is inside the subset that class retrieves.

β can be near 1 while α sits under `m / n`. That happens exactly when the observed exception is a symptom, a masker, or a handler's rewritten exception, and the root is upstream of the frame the class points at. Adding a bucket called `masked_crash` raises β if the text is distinctive. It raises α only when the bucket changes the operator used to build A. For the crash class, the operator that has a chance of raising α is the predecessor cone: `get_code_neighbors` on the crash frame with `called_by` and a small depth. The operator that looks faithful and often misses is "patch the line in the traceback."

The dossier's defensive-versus-guard fork is this distinction in operational form.

- Defensive hypothesis. The crash site is an invalid state that was produced earlier. A is the predecessor cone. The edit repairs the state.
- Guard hypothesis. The crash site is a missing check. A is the crash frame. The edit adds the guard.

Those are two causal models inside one observational class. They should be tried in that order for crashes, defensive first, because a guard at a symptom frame preserves the bad state and fails the pass-to-pass tests that still exercise it. They should not be two more names in a growing list of exception types. The pre-registered comparison is in section 8.

## 4. Fault masking, and the constructed redirect example

### 4.1 The definition the literature uses

Debroy and Wong, "Insights on Fault Interference for Programs with Multiple Bugs" (ISSRE 2009, DOI 10.1109/ISSRE.2009.14), study programs with two faults. Let P_f be the program that contains only fault f, and P_{f,g} the program that contains both. For a test t:

- Failure masking: t fails on P_f and passes on P_{f,g}. The added fault has removed the failure-causing effect of the first.
- A new collective failure: t passes on each single-fault program and fails on P_{f,g}.

On the Siemens suite, across the multi-fault versions they generated (3,267 data points in their Table 3 discussion), neither relation holds unconditionally. Failure masking is the more common of the two observations. The independence assumption, neither masking nor a new collective failure, holds about 33.12% of the time, so some interference is expected about 66.88% of the time. Those percentages are about one constructed suite of C programs. They are a reason to expect interference. They are not a base rate for this competition's Python issues.

Voas, "PIE: A Dynamic Failure-Based Technique" (IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering, 18(8), 1992, DOI 10.1109/32.153381), supplies the three atoms a failure needs. Later work states them as a conjunction: a fault is observed on a test only when that test executes the fault, the execution infects the state, and the infection propagates to an output the test checks. Masri and Abou Assi (ACM TOSEM, 23(1), 2014, DOI 10.1145/2559932) use that conjunction explicitly. Masking is the failure of one atom for one fault because another fault is present.

Write E(f, t), I(f, t), P(f, t) for those three atoms. The failure set of a fault set F is

```text
Fail(F) = { t : some f in F has E(f, t) and I(f, t) and P(f, t) }
```

Independence would say `Fail({f, g}) = Fail({f}) ∪ Fail({g})`. Masking and collective failure are the two ways that equality fails. In particular, reachability masking is the execution atom:

```text
E(g, t) is false while f is present, and true after f is removed.
```

Then g cannot appear in `Fail({f, g})` through test t, and it can appear in `Fail({g})`. The observed failure set is not a sufficient statistic for the fault set.

### 4.2 The constructed example, read as reachability masking

The dossier's `httpx_6821` story is a constructed pair of defects in `encode/httpx`. It is not a training task and not a hidden task. The pattern, which is the part worth keeping:

- Fault f is an assignment `history = []` on the scheme-changing redirect path inside `_send_handling_redirects`. Redirect responses are dropped.
- Fault g is a hard assert in `Response.stream` (`self._stream is not None`).
- While f drops those responses, the stream method is not executed on that path. `E(g, t)` is false. The assert does not fire. A test written for g stays green.
- After f is removed, the redirect response survives, `E(g, t)` becomes true, and the assert fires.

So `g ∈ Fail({g})` is possible while `g ∉ Fail({f, g})` for the tests that only reach `Response.stream` through the redirect path. An agent that classifies the issue by the exception it can see, and then patches the assert, has maximized observational fidelity and missed the predecessor. When a later test finally reaches the stream, the assert fix may itself be the wrong repair (a guard around a state bug), and the history bug is still present.

The dossier also contains a turn-by-turn telling in which the first pytest failure is the assert, and a comparison in which a naive agent spends 30 turns and a BCF agent spends 10. Those counts were withdrawn with the rest of draft 1's results, and that telling disagrees with reachability masking: if the assert is the first failure, then g is executed while f is still present. The next draft should use one causal story. This note uses reachability masking, because that is the story in which classification of the visible exception points at the wrong fault. The turn counts stay out.

A faithful repair order for this pattern is the partial order `g depends on f`. The source is f. The edit removes the history clear. The agent reruns. A newly failing assert at `Response.stream` is an unmasking event, predicted by `depends_on`, and the next edit replaces the assert with the exception the maintainer intended. A newly failing test in a file the spec marked out of scope is a regression, and gate G5 reverts.

The real training task `httpx_3672` does not illustrate this. Its gold patch renames `complete()` to `reset()` and changes the return. It should be labeled as a single-file contract change if the taxonomy agrees, and it should not be narrated as a masking chain.

### 4.3 What BCF can actually do about masking

The framework does not remove masking by having more exception classes. It removes the damage masking does to search, by three concrete policy choices.

1. The crash class builds A as the predecessor cone of the crashing frame, via `get_code_neighbors(..., called_by, depth=2)`, and only then considers a guard at the frame. That is how α stays above `m / n` when the root is upstream. The cone can be larger than a naive class subset. The threshold still applies: a depth-2 cone that covers half the repository fails the inequality even if it contains the root, because m is too big for the budget. Depth is a parameter to measure, starting at 2 because that is one caller plus the caller's caller.
2. The spec card stores `depends_on`. The first edit is a source of that partial order. The agent is forbidden to patch a successor while a listed predecessor is untouched. This is the minimum-path tenet applied to a chain.
3. After an edit, the residual failure set is interpreted with the partial order. Define `Fail_0` as the tests that fail on the original tree, and `Fail_i` as the tests that fail after the first i accepted edits. A test in `Fail_i \ Fail_{i-1}` whose stack matches a listed successor is unmasking: continue. A test in that difference whose stack is outside the spec scope is a regression: revert under G5. The naive policy treats every new failure as a reason to undo the edit or to patch whatever frame just appeared. That policy repairs the masker and then reverts the repair, which is the loop the constructed story was written to prevent.

None of these three reads `test_patch`. The issue text, the traceback if one is present, the graph, and the pytest output the agent itself produces are the inputs available at score time.

## 5. Fault interference when both faults are visible

Masking is the case where one fault hides another. Interference in the spectrum-based fault-localization literature is the case where both faults produce failures and those failures share code.

DiGiuseppe and Jones, "Fault density, fault types, and spectra-based fault localization" (Empirical Software Engineering, 20(4), 928–967, 2015; first online 18 March 2014; DOI 10.1007/s10664-014-9304-1), define fault-localization interference as a fault obstructing another fault's ability to be localized. Across more than 72,000 multiple-fault versions of six programs, the effect of adding faults was significant and small, a fault's localizability varied with which other faults were present, and four existing fault taxonomies did not predict the interference. At least one fault remained localizable at roughly single-fault effectiveness. The practical reading: the first fault an agent finds can be a real fault and still be the wrong one to fix first, and the type label on the fault is a weak guide to whether interference is happening.

The ranking those papers disturb is a spectrum score. With `a_ef` the number of failing tests that execute a site, `a_ep` the number of passing tests that execute it, and `a_nf` the number of failing tests that do not, the Ochiai coefficient used by Abreu, Zoeteweij, and van Gemund is

```text
Ochiai(s) = a_ef(s) / sqrt( (a_ef(s) + a_nf(s)) · (a_ef(s) + a_ep(s)) )
```

Two failing tests that share a callee both increment `a_ef` on that callee. Each fault's rank moves. A class partition that then searches "the" buggy area using the pooled failures mixes the two faults. Callaghan and Fischer, FLITSR (ISSTA 2023, arXiv:2306.09892; journal version in ACM TOSEM, 2025, DOI 10.1145/3745027), define masking in this ranking sense: an interaction that drops the rank of one fault because other program elements are present. Their repair is iterative test-suite reduction. Remove the failing tests that execute the current top element, re-rank, and the previously masked element rises. On their synthetic sets (up to 32 injected faults) and on 326 multi-fault Defects4J versions (up to 14 real faults), they report roughly 30% to 90% reductions in wasted effort relative to the best base metric they tried, with large variance across projects.

This agent does not have a coverage matrix, and it should not grow one inside a five-minute budget. The one-step version that fits the harness is the residual set in section 4.3. Pytest's fail-fast flag makes the situation sharper: the first failure is one element of `Fail(F)`, chosen by collection order. One-bug-at-a-time localization, as the handbook chapter of Gao, Wong, and Abreu describes it (Handbook of Software Fault Localization, chapter 12, Wiley-IEEE, 2023, DOI 10.1002/9781119880929.ch12), ranks from all current failures and fixes the top site. Under reachability masking, the top site is the one that can still execute. The hidden fault has no rank until the masker moves. Clustering failures into fault-focused groups (Jones, Bowring, and Harrold, "Debugging in Parallel", ISSTA 2007, DOI 10.1145/1273463.1273468; Gao and Wong, MSeer, IEEE TSE 45(3), 2019, DOI 10.1109/TSE.2017.2776912) has the same blind spot. A masked fault produces no cluster. Parallel debugging starts to apply after the first source fault is removed and two residual failures point at different successors. That is a sprint-5 rescue question, not the default architecture. The plan already keeps a single root unless a 10-task comparison says otherwise.

## 6. What the last sentence should say

The sentence "the speed and overall effectiveness of the algorithm will simply depend on the adjustments to the number and fidelity of the exception categories" is right about the arguments and wrong about the dependence.

For independent single faults, effectiveness is `C(m(K), α(K))`. Raising K shrinks the subset and, past a point, shrinks coverage. The class helps while `α > m / n`. Observational fidelity β is the quantity a confusion matrix on exception text will report. Causal coverage α is the quantity that enters C. Measuring β and calling it fidelity overstates the search benefit whenever faults mask each other.

For masking and interference, K is the wrong dial past a small value. The dials that change the outcome are the subset operator (crash frame versus caller cone), the partial order `depends_on`, and the reading of `Fail_i \ Fail_{i-1}` as unmasking or as regression. A larger catalog of exception names leaves those operators untouched.

The adjusted claim, which the draft prose below uses: speed and effectiveness depend on K and on causal coverage, they trade off, and once faults interact they also depend on the predecessor operator and the fix order. The number of exception categories is a small integer chosen by the ablation, not a free source of speed.

## 7. Prose for the next draft

The following section is written to be pasted into the next whitepaper draft. Receipt slots are marked `[R:…]`. Until a run fills them, the tag is H for the experimental claims and I for the constructed example. The algebra can be tagged D, derived, because it does not depend on a run.

---

### Class-conditional localization and the coverage threshold

We classify an issue from its text into a small set of classes and retrieve a subset A of the candidate root locations. Let n be the number of candidates in the unrestricted set, m the size of A, and α the probability that the true root lies in A. Inspecting in uniform order and falling back to the complement after a miss has expected cost

```text
C(m, α) = (m + 1) / 2 + (1 - α) · n / 2.
```

Unrestricted search costs `(n + 1) / 2`. The subset is strictly cheaper if and only if `α > m / n`. We call α causal coverage, and we distinguish it from observational fidelity, the probability that the predicted class matches the exception described in the issue. A crash label can be observationally perfect and still retrieve the wrong subset when the crashing frame is a symptom.

We therefore keep K small. The pre-registered comparison is K = 1 (no class), K = 3 (`crash`, `logic_or_contract`, `feature`, with `unknown` falling back to unrestricted search), and K = 4 (logic and edge case split). The classifier reads the issue text. The hidden tests are not an input. For `crash`, the retrieved set is the caller cone of the crashing frame, obtained from the graph neighbor tool at depth 2, and a guard at the frame is the second hypothesis rather than the first. We report m, α against the gold root file, expected inspections, and resolution on the development split `[R:sprint3-class-ablation]`. We adopt a K greater than 1 only when that arm resolves at least two more development tasks and does not drop the held-out repository.

### When the failure set is not the union of the faults

Classification assumes the observed failure points at the root. Multi-fault programs violate that assumption in two ways that the testing literature separates.

Fault masking (Debroy and Wong, ISSRE 2009): a test that fails for fault f can pass once fault g is added, because g removes the failure-causing effect of f. The complementary event is a collective failure, a test that fails only when both faults are present. On their Siemens multi-fault versions, independence held about a third of the time. We cite that figure as evidence that interference is ordinary in constructed multi-fault suites, not as a base rate for this benchmark.

The mechanism is the PIE conjunction (Voas, IEEE TSE, 1992). A fault is observed when a test executes it, the execution infects the state, and the infection propagates to a checked output. Reachability masking is the failure of the execution atom. We use a constructed example, disclosed as constructed, because the training set's single httpx task is not a chain. Call the example a scheme-changing redirect that clears `history`, composed with an assert inside `Response.stream`. While the clear is present, redirect responses are discarded and the assert is not executed. Removing the clear makes the assert observable. The failure set of the pair is not the union of the failure sets of the parts. An agent that patches the assert first has followed the visible exception into a successor fault.

Fault-localization interference (DiGiuseppe and Jones, Empirical Software Engineering, 2015) is the other case: both faults are observed, their failing tests share code, and a spectrum score such as Ochiai moves each fault's rank. Iterative removal of the tests that cover the current top site is the standard repair for that distortion (Callaghan and Fischer, FLITSR, ISSTA 2023). We do not build a coverage matrix inside the task budget. We keep the one-step residue. After accepted edits Φ, a test that newly fails is unmasking when its stack matches a successor listed in the specification's `depends_on` relation, and it is a regression when it falls outside the specification scope. Regressions revert. Unmasking continues down a topological order of that relation.

The effectiveness of the agent is therefore C(m(K), α(K)) while faults are independent, and it additionally depends on the predecessor operator and the fix order when they are not. Adjusting the number of exception categories moves only the first of those terms, and only inside the region `α > m / n`.

### Threats

The uniform-inspection model ignores rank quality inside A. If the localizer's ranking is strong, the benefit of a class shrinks, because unrestricted search was already cheap. The gold-root labels used to estimate α are an offline research artifact; the submitted agent does not receive them. The constructed redirect example illustrates reachability masking and is not a measured trajectory. A multi-file gold patch is not, by itself, evidence of masking. We mark `multi_bug` only when two defects are causally dependent, using the taxonomy protocol reported separately `[R:taxonomy-v1]`.

---

## 8. Pre-registered experiment

This is the measurement that turns section 7 from algebra into a result. It attaches to one controlled day in sprint 3 (2026-10-15 to 2026-10-21), on the development split of 30 tasks (18 fastapi, 12 rich), temperature 0, the same tool cap as the prompt-freeze candidate. The `requests` holdout is scored once at the end, under the plan's existing graduation rule.

Offline, before the day, record the gold root file of each development task from the gold patch. Those labels stay out of the prompt, the skills, and the training mixture.

| Arm | What the localizer is told | Subset operator |
| --- | --- | --- |
| K = 1 | Issue text, no class | Current symbol harvest and graph lookup |
| K = 3 | One of `crash`, `logic_or_contract`, `feature`, `unknown` | `crash` uses `called_by` depth 2 from the traceback frame. Other classes use symbol harvest. `unknown` uses the K = 1 operator |
| K = 4 | `logic_or_contract` split into `logic` and `edge_case` | Same operators as K = 3 |

For each task and arm, log the predicted class, m, whether the gold file is in the retrieved set, the number of inspections until the gold file is read or the budget ends, and whether the task resolves. Publish α, mean m, mean inspections, and resolves. The algebraic prediction to confirm or reject: K = 4 beats K = 3 only if its drop in m raises C by less than its drop in α costs, and any arm with α at or below m/n loses to K = 1 on inspections.

A paired comparison inside the crash-labeled tasks, same day if the slice is large enough and otherwise the next dev day: predecessor cone first, versus patch-the-frame first. Endpoint is gold-file hit, then resolution. This is the defensive-versus-guard test. It is a comparison of operators, not of extra class names.

Secondary endpoint, after the taxonomy has frozen: on tasks labeled `multi_bug` or tier 4, the rate of order errors, defined as a first edit in a successor file while a predecessor gold file is untouched. That rate is the empirical content of section 4.3. With 30 development tasks the resolution counts will be small. The pre-registered primary quantities are α and m, which the formula uses directly. Resolution stays the graduation rule for changing the submission, and it is reported with the counts rather than as a percentage carried to several places.

## 9. Prior research

This section stands on its own. It answers three questions: what has been published on fault interference and fault masking, including work that finds issues in repositories; where in a codebase those phenomena occur; and which part of that work transfers to this agent.

### 9.1 The honest gap

Published coding-agent papers that start from a GitHub issue — SWE-bench (Jimenez et al., ICLR 2024), SWE-agent (Yang et al., NeurIPS 2024), Agentless (Xia, Deng, Dunn, and Zhang, 2024), AutoCodeRover (Zhang, Ruan, Fan, and Roychoudhury, ISSTA 2024) — frame the job as producing one patch for one issue. They spend their method sections on search over files, editing, and test execution. Multi-file and multi-hunk patches appear as an outcome statistic. Those papers do not model fault masking, they do not define a fix partial order, and they do not use the interference vocabulary of the testing literature. A search for the solution to "GitHub issue agents under fault masking" comes back empty for a reason. The prior art that matches the statement is older, and it lives in fault localization, mutation testing, and crash bucketing. The agent papers matter as the engineering frame we have to fit that art into: one issue text, a short budget, a patch that must pass a hidden test suite.

There is a second gap inside the testing literature. Defects4J, the usual real-bug benchmark, is built mostly as one fault per version. Studies that need interference either inject several faults (the Siemens constructions, DiGiuseppe and Jones) or compose real faults after the fact (FLITSR's 326 multi-fault Defects4J versions). Thirty-eight of the 129 training gold patches touch two or more files. That is evidence of multi-location repair. It becomes evidence of masking only after someone checks the failure sets of the separated hunks. That check is part of the taxonomy, done offline, on training tasks.

### 9.2 Interference and masking

| Source | Phenomenon | Finding this draft relies on |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Debroy and Wong, ISSRE 2009, DOI 10.1109/ISSRE.2009.14 | Failure masking and collective failure | A test can pass when a second fault is added, or fail only when both are present. On their Siemens multi-fault versions, independence held about 33.12% of the time, and masking was the more common departure |
| Voas, IEEE TSE 1992, DOI 10.1109/32.153381 | PIE: execution, infection, propagation | A fault stays invisible until all three atoms hold. Reachability masking is the execution atom failing because another fault is present |
| Masri and Abou Assi, ACM TOSEM 2014, DOI 10.1145/2559932 | Coincidental correctness | The fault executes and the output is still right, because the infection never propagates. This is common, and it makes coverage-based ranks worse |
| DiGiuseppe and Jones, Empirical Software Engineering 2015, DOI 10.1007/s10664-014-9304-1 | Fault-localization interference | Other faults change how localizable a fault is. Fault type, under the taxonomies they tried, did not predict the interference |
| Jones, Bowring, and Harrold, ISSTA 2007, DOI 10.1145/1273463.1273468 | Parallel debugging | Failing tests can be clustered into fault-focused groups. Masked faults have no cluster until they can fail a test |
| Gao and Wong, MSeer, IEEE TSE 2019, DOI 10.1109/TSE.2017.2776912 | Due-to clustering | Grouping failing tests by which bug they are due to beats using the pooled suite. Evaluated on 840 multiple-bug versions of seven programs |
| Abreu, Zoeteweij, and van Gemund, ASE 2009, DOI 10.1109/ASE.2009.25 | Barinel, spectrum-based multiple-fault localization | A probabilistic model of several faults at once, as an alternative to fixing one and rerunning |
| Callaghan and Fischer, FLITSR, ISSTA 2023, arXiv:2306.09892 | Ranking masking | Iteratively dropping the failing tests of the current top element recovers faults whose rank was suppressed. Large gains, large project-to-project variance |
| Gao, Wong, and Abreu, Handbook of Software Fault Localization, ch. 12, 2023, DOI 10.1002/9781119880929.ch12 | One-bug-at-a-time | The sequential policy localizes whatever is observable now. Under masking, that is the fault that can still execute |
| Wong, Gao, Li, Abreu, and Wotawa, IEEE TSE 2016, DOI 10.1109/TSE.2016.2521368 | Survey | The map of slice-based, spectrum-based, and model-based localization through 2014. The right anchor citation for the related-work section |
| de Kleer and Williams, Artificial Intelligence 1987; Reiter, Artificial Intelligence 1987 | Diagnosis from a hypothesis set | The formal ancestor of "retrieve a subset that should contain the fault." Multiple-fault diagnoses are sets, not a single guilty component |
| Offutt, ACM TOSEM 1992; DeMillo, Lipton, and Sayward, IEEE Computer 1978 | Coupling effect | Tests that kill simple faults kill most complex faults often enough that testers treat higher-order faults as partly covered. This is why masking feels rare in ordinary testing. It does not imply that the failure set of a composition equals the union |
| Jia and Harman, higher-order mutation (SCAM 2008 and follow-on) | Subtle faults | A second mutation can mask a first, producing a mutant that is harder to kill than either parent. The software analogue of a successor edit hiding a predecessor |

Abreu, Zoeteweij, and van Gemund's practical evaluation of spectrum formulas (Journal of Systems and Software, 2009) is the citation for preferring Ochiai among the classical coefficients. Jones, Harrold, and Stasko (ICSE 2002) introduced the Tarantula visualization that this line of work grew out of. Zeller's delta debugging and the cause-transition work with Cleve (ICSE 2005) are the single-fault causal tools: they explain one failing run by narrowing inputs and state. They apply inside one fault. They do not order a pair of faults. The survey of Wong, Gao, Li, Abreu, and Wotawa is the right place to send a reader who wants that single-fault machinery.

### 9.3 Repository issues, crash buckets, and "what kind of issue is this"

The closest published relative of "classify the exception, then search a smaller space" is crash bucketing, not coding agents.

- Dang, Wu, Zhang, Zhang, and Nobel, ReBucket (ICSE 2012), cluster duplicate crash reports by call-stack similarity. The class is a bucket of stacks. The point of the bucket is to stop searching the same crash twice.
- Wu, Zhang, Cheung, and Kim, CrashLocator (ICSE 2014), take the crash stack as a query and rank functions that are likely to contain the crashing fault, including functions that are not themselves on the stack. That is the predecessor-cone idea in information-retrieval form, and it is the strongest single citation for the crash operator in section 3.
- Zhou, Zhang, and Lo, BugLocator (ICSE 2012, "Where Should We Fix This Bug?"), rank files from the words of a bug report. The issue text is a query. Our harness cannot embed that query for `search_similar_code`; it can take the symbols BugLocator-style methods would have weighted, and look those symbols up as keys. The harvest-then-lookup skill is the part of this literature the tool contract allows.
- Antoniol, Ayari, Di Penta, Khomh, and Guéhéneuc (CASCON 2008, "Is it a bug or an enhancement?") and Herzig, Just, and Zeller (ICSE 2013, "It's not a bug, it's a feature") study issue-type classification. The useful result for us is the error rate. Humans and classifiers disagree about bug versus enhancement often enough that a four-way split of bug subtypes, made from issue text alone, will be noisy. That noise is exactly a drop in α as K grows. Herzig, Just, and Zeller is the empirical warning against K = 4 unless the ablation measures α.

None of these papers solves a chained patch under a hidden test suite. CrashLocator in particular assumes a stack. Many of our training issues have no stack: the median issue is a few hundred characters, and a traceback is uncommon. The class `unknown` exists so those issues take the unrestricted operator instead of a pretended crash cone.

### 9.4 Where in a codebase interference and masking show up

The literature above, read for mechanism, names a short list of sites. These are the patterns the taxonomy codebook should include, as mechanism codes, if a human can see them in the gold patch and the issue. They are hypotheses about where to look, confirmed per task by the residual-failure check, not a detector we ship.

| Site | Mechanism | What the agent should do when the issue looks like this |
| --- | --- | --- |
| An early clear, return, or raise that skips later code | Reachability masking. The execution atom of the later fault is false | Put the early control-flow edit first in `depends_on`. Treat a new failure at the skipped site as unmasking |
| Shared mutable state, one write hiding another | Data-dependence masking. Infection from the second fault never happens, or is overwritten | Predecessor cone along writers of that name, using the graph and a symbol search, depth kept small enough that `m / n` stays believable |
| A faulty line that runs inside passing tests | Coincidental correctness. Propagation fails (Masri and Abou Assi) | A passing test that touches the edited line is weak evidence of innocence. G5 still reverts when a formerly passing test starts failing |
| Two failures whose stacks share a callee | Spectrum interference. Ochiai rises on the shared callee | Resist patching the shared callee just because both traces mention it. Prefer the frames that differ |
| `pytest` fail-fast, one failure shown | The visible fault is one element of `Fail(F)`, selected by order | The first frame is a hypothesis. The spec may name a predecessor the issue text describes and the frame does not |
| A defensive assert far from the bad write | The crash class is observationally right and causally downstream | Caller cone before a guard at the assert |
| A handler that catches one exception and raises another | The observed class is the handler's class | Follow `called_by` and the raise site into the handler before editing the original producer |
| A second edit that restores an output the first edit broke | Higher-order masking, as in subtle mutants | The partial order records which edit is the source. Reverting the source to "get back to green" re-hides the successor |

A practical identification procedure that uses only training-time artifacts, and that must not run inside the submitted agent:

1. Split a multi-hunk gold patch into single hunks.
2. Apply each hunk alone and apply the full patch, and run the same tests.
3. If a test fails on a single hunk and passes on the full patch, the other hunks mask that fault on that test.
4. If a test fails only on the full patch, the hunks form a collective failure, or the test is sensitive to their composition.
5. Record the outcome in the taxonomy as `multi_bug` with a one-sentence rationale.

That is Debroy and Wong's comparison, applied to a gold patch instead of to injected faults. It is how a real tier-4 example will be chosen to sit beside, or to replace, the constructed redirect story. It is also how we will avoid calling every multi-file patch a masking chain.

### 9.5 Which part is relevant here

Relevant, and used in the draft:

- The coverage threshold, as the formal version of "a class limits the search." Diagnosis theory supplies the hypothesis-set idea. The inequality `α > m / n` is the piece that corrects the monotone claim.
- PIE, as the vocabulary that makes reachability masking a precise event rather than a metaphor. The constructed redirect story is an execution-atom example.
- Debroy and Wong's qualitative result that masking is common in multi-fault compositions, cited with their suite named, so we do not pretend 66.88% is a property of fastapi.
- CrashLocator and BugLocator, as the justification for a caller cone and for symbol-shaped queries. Both match a tool we actually have.
- Herzig, Just, and Zeller, as the reason K stays at 3 until K = 4 wins a measured comparison on α and on resolves.
- The residual-set rule, as the one-step form of FLITSR and of one-bug-at-a-time that a five-minute agent can run. Newly appeared failures are interpreted with `depends_on` before they are allowed to revert a good predecessor fix.
- The taxonomy procedure in section 9.4, as the way this training set grows a few real chained examples without inventing another `httpx_6821`.

Relevant as background, and cited once so a reviewer can see we know it:

- Ochiai, Tarantula, Barinel, MSeer, and parallel debugging. They need spectra or a clusterable set of failing tests. We will not have those inside the task. DiGiuseppe and Jones's finding that fault type did not predict interference is itself an argument against a large exception taxonomy.
- The coupling effect. It explains the intuition that hidden faults are rare. Debroy and Wong are the counterweight. The draft states both and then measures `multi_bug` on this training set instead of picking a side from memory.
- The coding-agent papers. They define the task shape and the localize-then-edit baseline the plan already implements. They are not prior solutions of masking.

Not used as evidence:

- Any turn count, accuracy, or chart from whitepaper draft 1, or from the constructed `httpx_6821` trace.
- SWE-bench Verified headline numbers. Different harness, and `requests` overlaps our holdout.
- A prize split or a paper rubric. The local overview snapshot states the $35,000 pool, the four topics, and the 2026-11-12 23:59 UTC deadline.

## 10. Sources

Debroy, V., and Wong, W. E. Insights on Fault Interference for Programs with Multiple Bugs. ISSRE 2009, pp. 165–174. DOI 10.1109/ISSRE.2009.14.

Voas, J. M. PIE: A Dynamic Failure-Based Technique. IEEE TSE 18(8), 1992, pp. 717–727. DOI 10.1109/32.153381.

Masri, W., and Abou Assi, R. Prevalence of coincidental correctness and mitigation of its impact on fault localization. ACM TOSEM 23(1), Article 8, 2014. DOI 10.1145/2559932.

DiGiuseppe, N., and Jones, J. A. Fault density, fault types, and spectra-based fault localization. Empirical Software Engineering 20(4), 2015, pp. 928–967. DOI 10.1007/s10664-014-9304-1.

Gao, R., Wong, W. E., and Abreu, R. Software Fault Localization for Programs with Multiple Bugs. In Wong and Tse, eds., Handbook of Software Fault Localization, ch. 12, Wiley-IEEE, 2023. DOI 10.1002/9781119880929.ch12.

# Coderprog catalog: what helps a BCF agent win the Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition

*Research scope: Every record in `coderprog_catalog.json` (3,130 items) was screened for usefulness in training, improving, or testing a Batonic Coding Framework agent whose scored artifact is a `submission.zip` for the Google DeepMind Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition. The frame is the competition pages and harness notes stored in this repo, plus the local BCF compendium. A resource is useful when reading it changes the agent loop, the allowed LoRA post-training, or the pytest evaluation. As of 2026-09-30.*

*As of: 2026-09-30. The competition is open through 2 December 2026, 23:59 UTC, so the rules below are the local snapshots fetched 29 September 2026, not a later page edit.*

## Executive summary

Sixteen of the 3,130 coderprog records are worth studying for this competition. The highest leverage is language and test discipline that still applies when the hidden tasks are private repositories: Effective Python (86), the Python 3.13 object-oriented programming edition (84), the packaging-and-pytest course (82), Essential Test-Driven Development (81), and the DPO/GRPO alignment course (80). The catalog has no book whose title is pytest, httpx, requests, urllib3, Starlette, Rich, Pydantic, SWE-bench, or Git internals. FastAPI appears as greenfield application courses, which help the public `fastapi/fastapi` dev tasks and do not, by themselves, describe the hidden set.

Read these as the human designer. The winner's license is Apache 2.0 and external data has to be available to other competitors at no more than a modest cost, so these catalog files are a private reading list. They are not a corpus to paste into `skills/` or into a LoRA training set.

Confidence is high on the competition mechanism, because it is written in the local overview, data page, rules, and `HARNESS_README.md`. Confidence is moderate on each course, because the judgment uses the catalog synopsis rather than a second publisher page.

## What "useful" means here

The prediction score is the fraction of tasks whose patch applies and whose tests pass. The public development set has 129 tasks drawn from `fastapi/fastapi`, `Textualize/rich`, `psf/requests`, and `encode/httpx`. The hidden test set is about 120 tasks from private repositories, built with the same graph and pytest pipeline. The sandbox is Python 3.13. Phase 2 runs hermetic pytest and reads JUnit XML. The only base model allowed in a submission is `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`, optionally with PEFT LoRA adapters of rank at most 128, on vLLM across 4x L4 GPUs, with a 32,768-token context. The agent edits `/workspace` through a fixed tool list and calls `submit_patch()`.

The BCF agent, as specified in `bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md`, is a four-phase loop on that harness: specification, localization, a minimum patch, and a binary validation gate, with a rescue path after repeated failures. "Preserve working code", "minimum path", breadcrumbs, and machine-checkable done are the four tenets in that compendium. Parts of the compendium are marked reconstructed; the competition files are the scoring authority.

A record was ranked when its title or synopsis maps onto one of those mechanisms:

- **Test.** pytest, test-first development, fail-then-fix drills, JUnit output.
- **Improve.** Python 3.13, typing, asyncio, packaging, FastAPI or Pydantic as public-dev-set context, Git's diff and index, or an agent loop with tools, skills, and a stop condition.
- **Train.** LoRA, QLoRA, DPO, GRPO, or another post-training method that can be reimplemented on the allowed checkpoint.

Left out on purpose: generic prompt-engineering and RAG courses, Claude Code or Cursor product tours that never build a harness, offensive reverse-engineering and exploit material (115 records matched that screen), other-language stacks, and sales courses that only mention FastAPI as the HTTP wrapper around an unrelated chatbot. A first automated pass kept 68 records. Reading those synopses, plus a second pass over titles the automaton dropped, cut the list to the 16 below.

The catalog stores `publication_year` 1920 on 313 records. That value is a missing-year sentinel in this file, not a claim that the work was published in 1920. Where a ranked row shows 1920, the justification says so.

## How to read the scores

| Score | Meaning for this competition |
| --- | --- |
| 90-100 | Directly practices the grader or a public training library the grader runs: pytest, and pytest against FastAPI or httpx. |
| 80-89 | Transfers to private repos or to the allowed training loop: Python 3.13, packaging, asyncio, Pydantic, QLoRA or GRPO or DPO, test-first development. |
| 70-79 | Useful design input for the agent loop, Git diffs, or the public FastAPI dev set, with a clear part you should ignore. |
| 64-69 | Narrow slice only. Read the named chapter when a failure matches it. |

## Ranked resources

The table is sorted by `rank_score`, highest first. Every original catalog field follows the two added columns. Pipes and line breaks inside cells are escaped so the table stays one row per resource.

| rank_score | justification | source_url | title | post_date_raw | post_date_iso | category | category_slug | image_url | homepage_url | raw_meta_line | language | publication_year | isbn | pages | media_type | file_size | synopsis | extracted_at | missing_fields | extraction_status | local_image_path | unparsed_meta_segments |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 86 | Improve. Brett Slatkin's third edition is a language-level reference for reading and editing unfamiliar Python. The hidden test set is private repositories graded the same way as the public set, so idiom, pitfalls, and maintainable edits transfer further than a single-library tutorial. Use it while writing the patch-phase skill and while reviewing bad diffs on the 129 public tasks. | https://coderprog.com/effective-python-software-development-3rd/ | Effective Python: 125 Specific Ways to Write Better Python, 3rd Edition | January 7, 2026 | 2026-01-07 | Books | books | https://coderprog.com/wp-content/uploads/effective-python-software-development-3rd.jpg | https://coderprog.com/goto/r8WpYvcaxHZKggra3N4kldY1qFuxpni7RLkyYETFxWitbYAmhToy1-8TVNi_cTseEJIMsOZGpG7TeZYueBj7cso360i0Y7O4q2am | English \| 2025 \| ISBN: 978-0138172183 \| 672 Pages \| PDF, EPUB \| 96 MB | English | 2025 | 978-0138172183 | 672 | ["PDF", "EPUB"] | 96 MB | Master the art of Python programming with 125 actionable best practices to write more efficient, readable, and maintainable code. Python is a versatile and powerful language, but leveraging its full potential requires more than just knowing the syntax. Effective Python: 125 Specific Ways to Write Better Python, 3rd Edition is your comprehensive guide to mastering Python’s unique strengths and avoiding its hidden pitfalls. This updated edition builds on the acclaimed second edition, expanding from 90 to 125 best practices that are essential for writing high-quality Python code. Drawing on years of experience at Google, Brett Slatkin offers clear, concise, and practical advice for both new and experienced Python developers. Pretraining gives LLMs capability but not judgment. In Reinforcement Learning for LLM Alignment and Reasoning, youll learn how reinforcement learning techniques like DPO and GRPO shape model behavior, safety, and reasoning, and how to build the evaluation and governance systems that keep alignment on track. Learn How To - Distinguish between instructional, behavioral, style, and value alignment - Build and evaluate reward models using pairwise comparisons and Bradley-Terry - Apply DPO, GRPO, and other modern post-training techniques - Design evaluation harnesses to monitor safety, toxicity, and model drift - Implement a constitutional AI pipeline with self-critique and revision Lesson 1: Foundations of Alignment Lesson 1 sets the stage by defining what alignment is, explaining why modern LLMs need it, and exploring what can go wrong when models are not aligned properly. Sinan breaks down the four major forms of alignment: instructional, behavior, style, and value. He presents concrete examples of each, including how models can become too agreeable, too cautious, or outright unsafe. Sinan introduces the core problem in alignment: pretraining does not produce alignment. The lesson then turns to the three stages of modern LLM development: pretraining, supervised post-training, and preference post-training. You will explore how each phase shapes a models capability and potential risks. Finally, Sinan highlights calibration as a key foundation of trustworthy reasoning and previews how post-training techniques fit into a broader alignment pipeline. Lesson 2: Reinforcement Learning Basics for LLMs Lesson 2 goes under the hood of reinforcement learning, explaining how it can shape model behavior through trial and error. The lesson maps traditional reinforcement learning concepts such as agent, environments, actions, rewards, and exploration on to the world of large language models and breaks down the reinforcement loop that powers modern alignment. Sinan also covers why exploration models matter for LLMs, what happens when you explore too little or even too much, and how sampling can control and affect training stability. From there he discusses the classic algorithm that anchored early reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) efforts and proximal policy optimization (PPO), walking through how it works at a conceptual level. By the end of the lesson, you will have the foundation to understand how modern RLHF emerged, how it will evolve beyond PPO, and how that evolution will unlock better alignment and better reasoning. Lesson 3: Building a Reward Model Lesson 3 dives into the key ingredient that makes reinforcement learning LLM models work at scale: reward model. The lesson covers what a reward model is, how it is trained, and why it is essential to capture things like helpfulness, harmfulness, groundedness, tone, and value alignment. Sinan explores how pair-wise comparisons and the Bradley-Terry laws can help create a reward model and help it learn which outputs you prefer and which ones you dont. Reward models learn preferences, not truth. Next comes evaluating reward models with measures like pair-wise accuracy and Kendalls T to ensure that these reward models are reliable before using them in a reinforcement loop. By the end of this lesson, you will have a clear understanding of how reward models function and how they influence everything that follows in the alignment process. Lesson 4: Modern Post-Training In Lesson 4, Sinan looks at how reinforcement learning for alignment has evolved. He starts by examining why PPO, the algorithm behind early RLHF systems like InstructGPT, became difficult to scale and maintain. From there he explores two major families of modern post-training techniques. The first is offline preference optimization, which includes methods like direct preference optimization (DPO). ## How to use the sixteen

Start with the testing and Python rows before any FastAPI course. The hidden tasks are private repositories, so a method that only teaches you to scaffold a new FastAPI app improves the public dev set and leaves the leaderboard dependent on whether those private repos happen to look like FastAPI. Effective Python, the 3.13 OOP edition, packaging, and the two TDD resources are the ones that still apply on an unseen Python tree.

Use the alignment course and the QLoRA course only after the agent loop can already solve a slice of the 129. A LoRA cannot invent a localization policy the scaffold never asks for, and the reward has to be the harness pass or fail. The vLLM book and the Gemma serving notes in `HARNESS_README.md` are the constraint set for that run: one base model, rank at most 128, unpacked submission under 3 GiB, thinking budget inside 32,768 tokens.

Build Your Own Claude Code and the Codex CLI book are design references for the loop, skills, and sub-agents. The submission compiler accepts YAML, prompts, skills, and adapters. It does not accept a Python entrypoint, and it does not run Claude or Codex.

## Open questions

The private test repositories are not named. Library-specific scores for the FastAPI courses would drop if the hidden set is far from ASGI and HTTP clients, and they would rise if it is more of the same. This catalog cannot resolve that.

No synopsis here was checked against the publisher's current table of contents. Ed Donner's QLoRA project, the DeepSeek book, and the vLLM book may spend only a chapter on the technique the score depends on.

`Essential Test-Driven Development` and `Reinforcement Learning for LLM Alignment and Reasoning` have thin or truncated synopses in the catalog. The methods named (test-first development, DPO, GRPO) are explicit. Lesson-level quality is not.

Whether a reconstructed BCF phase machine beats a single agent with a strict skill is an experiment the compendium already flags. These books do not settle it.
