# 0day / Prizrak catalog: what helps a BCF agent win the Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition

*Research scope: Every record in `0dayprizrak_catalog.json` (50,848 items) was screened with the same question as the coderprog pass: which books and courses are useful for training, improving, or testing a Batonic Coding Framework agent aimed at a winning `submission.zip` in the Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition. Judgments use this repo's competition snapshots, the harness README, the BCF compendium, and the catalog synopses. As of 2026-09-30.*

*As of: 2026-09-30. Competition facts are from the local pages saved 29 September 2026. The close date on those pages is 2 December 2026, 23:59 UTC.*

## Executive summary

This catalog is the more useful of the two for the scored loop. Thirty-eight records clear the bar. The top of the list is pytest itself: a 50-lab pytest guide whose synopsis includes FastAPI endpoint tests and async tests with httpx (93), and a dedicated Modern Python Testing with pytest course (91). Behind those are Effective Python (86), two copies of a Pydantic course (84), a full asyncio course (83), and a fine-tuning course that names LoRA, QLoRA, DPO, and GRPO (82).

The same constraints apply as on the other list. The score is the fraction of hidden tasks whose patch passes pytest. The public 129 tasks are from FastAPI, Rich, requests, and httpx. The hidden set is about 120 tasks from private repositories. The only model is `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`, with optional LoRA. These files are a reading list for the person designing the agent. They are not public external data, and they should not be copied into the submission or into the training corpus. Winner submissions are licensed Apache 2.0, and external data has to be something other participants can obtain at modest cost.

Several rows are duplicate uploads of one course. They share a score. Keep the archive that actually contains the lessons named in the justification. File-host URLs that appeared inside synopses were removed from this table; the catalog id and `source_url` still identify the record. `download_links` from the JSON is omitted here because that field is a list of outbound file hosts, not a description of the work.

## What "useful" means here

The agent that can win this competition localizes a bug or feature in a Python repository, makes a minimum diff, and submits it only after the relevant tests pass. The BCF write-up in `bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md` names that as specification, localization, patch, and validation, with rescue after repeated failures. The harness implements validation as pytest plus JUnit XML, in a Python 3.13 container, with no network. Tools include file edits, `run_command`, `submit_patch`, and three code-graph queries. `git diff HEAD` is the patch.

Screening used those facts, not topic similarity to "AI" or "agents". An automated pass kept 515 records and marked 1,046 as offensive-security or reverse-engineering from the title and the opening of the synopsis. Those 1,046 were not ranked: the scored work is functional Python change, graded by tests. A hand reading of the high-precision remainder kept the 38 rows below.

Name collisions were dropped when the hit was a person, not a model. "Gemma Weir", "Gemma James", and "Gemma Bonham-Carter" are in the catalog and have nothing to do with Gemma 4. A wireless "LoRa" cookbook was not treated as a LoRA fine-tuning book.

The inclusion floor is 64. Below that, the overlap with the harness is a single buzzword.

## How to read the scores

| Score | Meaning for this competition |
| --- | --- |
| 90-100 | Directly practices the grader or a public training library the grader runs: pytest, and pytest against FastAPI or httpx. |
| 80-89 | Transfers to private repos or to the allowed training loop: Python 3.13, packaging, asyncio, Pydantic, QLoRA or GRPO or DPO, test-first development. |
| 70-79 | Useful design input for the agent loop, Git diffs, or the public FastAPI dev set, with a clear part you should ignore. |
| 64-69 | Narrow slice only. Read the named chapter when a failure matches it. |

Why This Course Most AI courses stop at "here's how to call an API", leaving you stuck when outputs are unreliable, knowledge is outdated, or a general-purpose model doesn't fit your domain. This course takes a different path. We bridge the gap between knowing how models work and building systems that work in production. You'll master the four techniques that separate AI engineers from API users, prompt engineering, RAG, supervised fine-tuning, and alignment, working ground-up from core principles so your skills stay relevant as tools and frameworks evolve. Course Modules Module 1: Large Language Models (Review from Course 1) Refresh and solidify the LLM foundations that everything else builds on. This module revisits how modern LLMs generate text-scaling laws, pre-training, SFT, and preference alignment-and takes you hands-on with decoding strategies and hallucination inspection, ensuring you're ready to engineer these models rather than just use them. Module 2: Prompt Engineering Master the craft of communicating with LLMs. Move from prompting fundamentals through proven engineering techniques to advanced strategies, learning to design prompts that consistently produce high-quality, reliable outputs across any application. Module 3: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Build AI systems that combine LLMs with external knowledge. Walk through every stage of the RAG pipeline-ingestion, retrieval, and synthesis-then learn to rigorously evaluate your system, enabling responses that are accurate, up-to-date, and verifiable. Module 4: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) Transform general-purpose models into specialized experts. Explore the limits of in-context learning, the fundamentals of fine-tuning, and efficient methods like LoRA and QLoRA (PEFT)-mastering datasets and training dynamics to adapt models to your domain without massive compute. Module 5: Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) Shape model behavior to match human preferences. Understand why alignment matters, the reinforcement learning fundamentals behind it, and the full RLHF workflow-including PPO, DPO, and GRPO alignment algorithms. What you’ll learn ⚡ Use AI tools such as GitHub Copilot to support structured code review work. ⚡ Review inherited codebases efficiently using AI assistance ⚡ Create reusable prompts for code review, feature review, risk triage, and modernization planning. ⚡ Summarize code structure and trace feature workflows with AI assistance. ⚡ Identify hidden business rules, role assumptions, validation behavior, and undocumented workflow expectations. ⚡ Build a technical debt register that separates evidence, impact, severity, and recommended action. ⚡ Use AI to identify security, privacy, access control, dependency, and modernization readiness concerns. ⚡ Generate and review unit, integration, browser-level, and manual tests. ⚡ Refactor small code seams safely using AI assistance. ⚡ Prepare useful pull request summaries and AI-assisted review packages. ⚡ Review AI-generated modernization plans before accepting, revising, or rejecting them. ⚡ Apply AI responsibly by verifying findings through code, tests, behavior, and human judgment. Requirements ❗ Basic C# and .NET experience. ❗ Basic familiarity with ASP.NET Core MVC or web application structure is helpful. ❗ Basic Git and GitHub knowledge. ❗ Ability to open, build, and run a .NET project using Visual Studio, Visual Studio Code, Rider, or the .NET CLI. ❗ Access to GitHub Copilot or a similar AI coding assistant is recommended for hands-on practice. ❗ No advanced AI, cybersecurity, or legacy modernization experience is required. Description AI coding tools can generate suggestions quickly. Professional code review still depends on context, evidence, testing, and judgment. When you inherit an older application, the risky parts are rarely obvious from the first file you open. Business rules may be hidden in controllers, views, services, JavaScript, validation logic, database assumptions, role checks, and years of small maintenance decisions. AI-Powered Code Review: Find Bugs, Debt, and Risks is designed to help you use AI as a practical review partner while keeping you in control of the engineering decisions. You will learn how to review an inherited application using a structured workflow that helps you understand the existing system before making changes, identify meaningful risks before they become production problems, and modernize code with better evidence and less guesswork. This course is for developers who already know AI can help write code but want a stronger process for code review. Many AI coding workflows focus on speed: generate a feature, accept a change, move on. That approach can be dangerous when the codebase already has hidden behavior, weak tests, unclear ownership, outdated dependencies, inconsistent validation, or fragile business workflows. In this course, you will practice a review-first approach that treats AI output as a starting point for investigation rather than a final answer. You will work through a realistic inherited application scenario and learn how to build a baseline before making changes. That baseline matters. Before refactoring, rewriting, or modernizing a feature, you need to know what currently works, what assumptions the system depends on, what user roles are expected to do, and where behavior might break. The course walks you through environment setup, database setup, project execution, and baseline assessment, so your review begins with working evidence rather than assumptions. From there, you will configure AI for serious review work. You will explore GitHub Copilot review features, repository instructions, reusable prompt libraries, and structured review prompts that produce more useful output. The goal is to make AI responses easier to verify, compare, and turn into review artifacts. Take the loop and the permission boundary. | 0daydown-agentic-automation-a-pytest-framework-with-claude-code | 0daydown.com | https://www.0daydown.com/09/3640403.html | 2026-09-30T12:55:59-04:00 | ok | Agentic Automation — A Pytest Framework with Claude Code |  | 16小时前 | 16 hours ago | 2026-09-29T20:55:59-04:00 | relative:zh:hours | 3.3 GB | eLearning | English |  |  |  |  | Agentic Automation: Skills, Subagents, Planning, and Test Automation What you’ll learn ⚡ Design and author purpose-built Claude Code subagents — giving each a narrow mandate and exactly the tools it needs ⚡ Separate thinking from doing with plan mode — produce a reviewable plan before writing code ⚡ Orchestrate a multi-agent engineering loop — plan → build → review → test — where a lean orchestrator routes work across agents ⚡ Turn requirements into a running Pytest + Playwright framework — mapping user stories and acceptance criteria into non-flaky automated tests ⚡ Work within enforceable guardrails and improve agents from real results — building inside allow/deny permission policies Requirements ❗ Comfortable with Python, Pytest, and Git; some Playwright and Claude Code exposure helps Description Agentic Automation Course Build a test-automation framework where the work is done by AI agents, not scripts you run by hand. Most automation courses teach you to write scripts. This one teaches you to build ateam — a roster of purpose-built Claude Code subagents that plan, write, review, run, and debug tests by reasoning about the work, using their own tools, and handing off to each other in a real engineering loop. You’ll author each specialist from scratch and wire it into a working pipeline ✅plan-architect researches a task and returns a reviewable plan before a line of code is written ✅automation-writer turns user stories and acceptance criteria into non-flaky Pytest + Playwright tests ✅senior-code-reviewer reviews the diff read-only and opens the PR on approval ✅test-suite-planner decomposes a story into prioritised, independent suites as Jira sub-tasks ✅test-runner executes each reviewed suite and posts a sanitized verdict ✅qa-failure-investigator tells a test bug from a product bug — byattempting the fix — and files evidence-backed defects A leanorchestrator routes work between them and carries context across the hand-offs. Because the heavy lifting stays inside each agent’s isolated context window, the orchestrator runs a long, multi-stage workflow without exhausting its own — in one measured build it held at~5% context used while ~250K tokens of work happened inside the subagents. What makes it different ✅“Agentic” is literal. Each agent is an autonomous LLM worker with a narrow mandate, its own context, and exactly the tools it needs — toolsare the capability boundary, so a read-only reviewer physically can’t mutate the repo. ✅Dogfooded throughout. Every agent is exercised on real work in the course’s own repo, driving a live Jira board and a real browser against a running app. Whatever breaks gets fed back into that agent’s prompt — so you learn to improve agents the way you’ll actually improve them. ✅Guardrails you workwith . Destructive, staging, commit, push, and PR-create commands are denied outright; sanctioned actions are wrapped in allow-listed skills. You learn to build inside enforceable policy, not around it. The pipelineplan → build → review → test, with a defect fork when a suite catches a real product bug. Progress is visible on the Jira board through a single parent-story status, one owner per transition To Do → In Planning → Ready for Dev → In Progress → In Review → In Testing → Done You’ll practice Matching tools to intent · planning before doing · delegating execution to keep an orchestrator’s context lean · separating “it compiles” from verified and “review” from security audit · and feeding real findings back into agents so each dogfood failure pre-empts its whole class of bug next time. Stack: Python · Pytest · Playwright · pipenv · pytest-html + Allure · custom Claude Code subagents and skills · live Jira Cloud. Educational course material — designed to be worked through interactively with Claude Code against a sandbox Jira project and a disposable app-under-test. I have a confession. I don't build the way I teach. For years, you've watched me code projects step-by-step. Every line typed by hand. Clean. Sequential. Great for learning. But that's not how I work anymore. Today, I explain what I want to an AI agent - the same way I'd explain it to you in a video - and it builds everything. I architect, steer, and review. The agent handles the implementation. The explanation is the code. Not vibe coding. Not copy-pasting ChatGPT. A real engineering workflow where AI agents write, test, and ship production code while I make the decisions that matter. This cohort teaches you that workflow by building the tool that powers it. The tutorial gapHere's the problem with coding tutorials - including mine 01They show the finished path. Every step planned, every file in place. Real development doesn't work that way.02They hide the real workflow. The one where AI agents do the heavy lifting and you do the engineering.You can follow along with any tutorial and build a full-stack app. But can you build one from a blank terminal, steering an AI agent as your only teammate? Still on the main public repository's stack. | prizrak-2245212 | prizrak.ws | https://prizrak.ws/viewtopic.php?id=2245212 | 2026-09-02T12:30:50-04:00 | ok | FastAPI Masterclass | FastAPI Masterclass | 2026-08-10 18:58:36 | 2026-08-10 18:58:36 | 2026-08-10T18:58:36-04:00 | absolute:datetime | 43.29 GB | eLearning | English (US) | mp4 |  |  |  | Welcome to FastAPI Masterclass , a comprehensive introduction to backend development in Python with FastAPI. FastAPI is a Python framework for building fast, modern server-side applications and APIs. It has exploded in popularity in the last few years. Today, it has more than 100,000 stars on GitHub and more daily downloads than Django and Flask on PyPi. It powers applications in companies like Netflix, Microsoft, Uber, OpenAI, and Amazon. One of FastAPI's greatest strengths is its flexibility. Unlike larger frameworks that impose a specific structure, FastAPI gives developers the freedom to choose how their applications are organized. You can customize the folder structure, database, ORM, authentication system, validation rules, testing strategy, and architecture. No previous backend development experience is required. This course follows a linear progression, beginning with the fundamentals and gradually advancing to topics such as authorization, authentication, testing, architecture, and more. Topics covered: Clients and servers Routes and HTTP methods Path parameters Query parameters Debugging with Visual Studio Code Cookies and Headers FastAPI Validator Functions (Path, Query, Cookie, Header, and More) Dependencies (Functions, Classes, and Other Callables) and Dependency Injection (DI) Object-Relational Mappers (ORM) Database Queries Database Migrations Codebase Structure Password hashing JSON Web Tokens (JWTs) Authentication Authorization and more! In addition to FastAPI, the course covers many helpful libraries and tools from the modern Python ecosystem including: uv Pydantic SQLModel SQLAlchemy SQLite 3 Alembic The first part of the course offers a step-by-step walkthrough of a rent-a-room project inspired by home-stay platforms such as Airbnb. We begin from scratch and gradually incorporate all the critical components of a modern FastAPI application. After the main project, the course explores common backend topics in dedicated sections. These include database migrations, password hashing, JWT-based authentication, authorization, application architecture, and automated testing. By the end of FastAPI Masterclass , you will not only understand the syntax and features of FastAPI but also understand the fundamental backend principles that apply across programming languages, libraries, or frameworks. I'm excited to teach you everything I know about this powerful framework, and I look forward to seeing you in the course! Useful if those two are missing a threading or process chapter you hit in a test failure. | prizrak-2205602 | prizrak.ws | https://prizrak.ws/viewtopic.php?id=2205602 | 2026-07-06T20:00:40-04:00 | ok | The PYTHON CONCURRENCY GUIDE | The PYTHON CONCURRENCY GUIDE: Build Faster Apps Using Threads, Processes, and Real-World Patternsby FRANKLIN SHEEP | 2026-05-26 16:00:26 | 2026-05-26 16:00:26 | 2026-05-26T16:00:26-04:00 | absolute:datetime | 109 MB |  |  | pdf |  |  |  | Are you tired of slow, single-threaded Python applications that can't keep up with today's demanding workloads? In "Master Concurrency in Python: Build Faster Apps Using Threads, Processes, and Real-World Patterns," you'll unlock the secrets to supercharging your code, making it run circles around traditional sequential programs. This comprehensive guide is your roadmap to mastering concurrency, drawing from proven techniques used in high-performance systems like web servers, data pipelines, and AI models-much like the practical focus in best-sellers such as "Python Concurrency with asyncio" by Matthew Fowler and "Learning Concurrency in Python" by Elliot Forbes.Start with the fundamentals in Chapter 1, where you'll grasp why concurrency is essential for modern apps, exploring Python's built-in tools to handle multiple tasks efficiently. Dive deeper into threads in Chapters 2 and 3, learning to create, synchronize, and optimize them with real-world examples like I/O-bound operations that slash execution times. Overcome the infamous Global Interpreter Lock (GIL) in Chapters 4 and 5, mastering multiprocessing patterns, pools, and shared memory to achieve true parallelism for CPU-intensive tasks-think accelerating machine learning computations or batch processing.Transition to the future of Python with asyncio in Chapters 6 and 7, harnessing coroutines, event loops, and async/await for non-blocking I/O that powers scalable web apps and APIs. Chapter 8 teaches you to blend threads, processes, and asyncio for hybrid solutions tailored to your needs. Then, apply it all in Chapter 9's case studies, from optimizing web scrapers to building efficient data streams, incorporating patterns that address common pitfalls like race conditions.Go beyond basics in Chapter 10 with performance tuning and debugging, using profiling tools to measure gains-expect up to 10x speedups in your projects. Secure your code in Chapter 11, with best practices to avoid deadlocks and ensure thread-safety. Explore advanced futures and executors in Chapter 12, preparing you for cutting-edge applications.The heart of the book lies in Chapter 13's hands-on projects: Build a multi-threaded file processor, an async API client, and a parallel data analyzer, complete with code snippets you can deploy immediately. Test everything robustly in Chapter 14, and conclude in Chapter 15 with forward-looking strategies to stay ahead in Python's evolving ecosystem.Packed with diagrams, benchmarks, checklists, and over 50 code examples, this book transforms theory into action. Whether you're a mid-level developer optimizing legacy code or a pro tackling complex systems, you'll gain the skills to build faster, more resilient apps. Don't let slow code hold you back-grab your copy now and start building the high-performance Python applications you've always envisioned. ## What this catalog adds beyond the other one

Coderprog does not contain a pytest course, a Pydantic course, an asyncio course, or a Git-internals course. This catalog does. If you study only one item from either file, study the pytest complete guide (id `0daydown-pytest-complete-guide-python-unit-testing-with-50-exercises`), because it is the grader and it also touches FastAPI and httpx.

The training block to actually implement is `prizrak-2255771` (and its twin `prizrak-2256435`): LoRA, QLoRA, DPO, and GRPO in one syllabus. The four uploads of Ed Donner's core track are a weaker QLoRA source, because the capstone is price prediction and several projects are unrelated apps. The RLHF book `prizrak-2085778` is the one that names Hugging Face TRL, PPO, and DPO, which is a concrete library path for a preference run. Point the reward at harness pass or fail.

FastAPI rows are capped in the 70s on purpose. They help you read the public `fastapi/fastapi` tasks. The data page says the test repositories are private. Pydantic and asyncio rank higher than another "build a REST API" course because validation and the event loop show up inside those tasks and also transfer to other Python services.

Git internals (`prizrak-2103236`) is the right reference when a patch is empty, when `pytest.ini` or `conftest.py` leaks into the diff, or when a new file never appears. The harness commits the generated pytest config before the agent starts, and `submit_patch` is `git add -N` plus `git diff HEAD`.

## Left on the shelf after a title-level look

These FastAPI-titled records were seen during the full-catalog pass and were not given a row. They are further greenfield API, deployment, or chatbot projects after the workbook, the masterclass, and the clean-architecture course were already ranked. A later pass can promote one if its files turn out to be a deeper treatment of dependency injection, testing, or Starlette than those three.

| Title as catalogued | Why it is not in the ranked table |
| --- | --- |
| Hands-On APIs for AI and Data Science Python Development with FastAPI | Title frames FastAPI as a data-science API wrapper. |
| Deploy FastAPI React PostgreSQL Apps on Amazon Cloud AWS | Deployment on AWS. The grader never sees the deploy step. |
| Python FastAPI: Complete Course - Build a Full-Stack Web App | Greenfield full-stack course. The 10-project workbook already covers that use. |
| Build Social App (FastAPI - React TS - gRPC - WebSocket) | Product build. WebSocket and gRPC are incidental. |
| Full Stack AI Mastery: Python + React + FastAPI + LangChain | FastAPI is the serving layer for a LangChain app. |
| FastAPI with GenAI and AgenticAI project From Basic to AI | Generative-AI product project. |
| Build a Production-Ready FastAPI AI Apps | Same serving-layer pattern. |
| E-Commerce App with Payment using FastAPI and Angular, React, or Vue | Three payment-app variants. Billing UI is outside the metric. |
| The Agnostic AI Gateway Orchestrating LLM Flows in FastAPI | Gateway product, not library repair. |
| Python Microservices with FastAPI (Early Access) | Synopsis contains no description of the book, only a file-host note. |

## Open questions

Duplicate ids may be the same archive under two scrapes. The table does not open the files, so "same course" means the synopsis matches, not that the bytes match.

`prizrak-2087022` (Essential Test-Driven Development, Early Release) has no descriptive synopsis. The score assumes it is the book described under that title in the coderprog catalog.

The pytest complete guide's httpx and FastAPI labs are known only from its synopsis. If those labs are a short final module, the course is still the best pytest resource here and the 93 would be a point or two high.

Rich, requests, urllib3, and Starlette still have no dedicated title in this catalog. Bugs in those three public repos have to be learned from the repositories themselves and from the general Python, pytest, asyncio, and Git rows.

The private test set's domain is unpublished. Every FastAPI and Pydantic score is conditional on the public dev set, and only partly conditional on the leaderboard.

# FOSS tools worth using for the BCF coding agent

*Research scope: Every GitHub repository and research note under `foss_tools` was inventoried and judged for one use: raising the pass rate of a BCF coding agent on the Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition. The agent is the declarative ADK submission described in this repo's Kaggle pages and `HARNESS_README.md`: one base model, `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`, optional PEFT LoRA adapters, skills, and the nine harness tools. BCF, as written in `bcf/BCF-Reference-Compendium.md`, is the four-phase loop of specification, localization, a minimum patch, and a binary validation. The score is the fraction of tasks whose patch applies and whose pytest run passes. A resource is useful when it trains that behavior, improves the prompts and skills, or tests them. Done means a ranked table of those resources, with the collection's metadata, a score, and a justification.*

*As of: 2026-09-30. Star counts and solve rates move quickly. Figures below are the collection's own numbers unless the row says they were re-checked against the live README on this date.*

## Executive summary

Forty-four resources in this collection clear a usefulness bar of 56. The remaining catalog, a bit over 4,200 repositories, stays below that bar for this contest. The single best pattern to copy is Agentless: localize to a file, then a symbol, then an edit, then keep the patch only when tests agree. The single best training stack in the folder is SWE-smith's task and trajectory data, fine-tuned with Unsloth or PEFT plus TRL into a safetensors LoRA, then scored with the competition harness on the 129 public tasks before anyone trusts a SWE-bench number.

Confidence is high on that filter and on the mechanism match. Confidence is medium on star counts that were not re-counted today, and on every solve-rate printed in a README. The hard gap is that no file in `foss_tools`, and none of the six READMEs re-read today, documents a LoRA recipe for `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`. Every training row assumes that gap gets closed before the adapter is worth uploading.

## How the score was built

Each resource received six factor scores from 0 to 5. The weighted sum is the published score, and the maximum is 100.

| Factor | Weight | 5 means | 1 means |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Metric match | 5 | A git patch is accepted only when pytest passes on a real repo issue | The repo is about models in general |
| Submission fit | 4 | Produces a PEFT adapter, a SKILL.md, or a procedure that maps onto the nine tools | A product that cannot be expressed in agent.yaml, prompts, or skills |
| Gemma 4 and harness fit | 4 | Works with gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct, 32,768 tokens, air-gap, rank at most 128 | Needs another base model, the network, or a tool the harness does not have |
| Evidence | 2 | Collection API figures plus a README re-read on 2026-09-30 | Social star counts, a generated synopsis, or an unreproduced leaderboard line |
| Actionability | 3 | A small team can use it on the way to the 2 December 2026 deadline | Archived, unlicensed, or documented only as a multi-node RL job |
| Distribution match | 2 | Python library issues in the style of the 129 public tasks | Another language, another modality, or function-level puzzles |

The main table keeps resources at 56 and above. A short second table lists near-misses whose indicative scores fall below that line. Equal scores are ordered by resource name.

## What the competition will actually accept

The overview and data pages in this repo, snapshotted 29 September 2026, define the job as Python bug fixes and feature requests. The public development file has 129 tasks from `fastapi/fastapi`, `Textualize/rich`, `psf/requests`, and `encode/httpx`, each with a problem statement, a gold patch, and a `test_patch`. The hidden test is about 120 tasks from private repositories, built so the new tests fail before the fix and pass after it. The agent has 12 hours for every task, including sandbox setup and excluding grading.

`HARNESS_README.md` fixes the artifact. The zip compiles to an ADK agent. Python entry points are rejected. Tools are the nine harness tools plus sub-agents declared as `agent_tool`. LoRA weights are PEFT safetensors, at most rank 128, and the unpacked zip must stay under 3 GiB. The server is vLLM on 4x L4 with a 32,768-token context and the Gemma 4 tool-call parser. Container B applies the agent's diff, restores test files, applies the official test patch, and runs pytest with no network.

That is why a popular coding agent can still score poorly here. If it cannot be rewritten as prompts, skills, and a PEFT adapter on that one checkpoint, it does not move the leaderboard.

## What to do with the top of the table

The improve rows and the train rows are two different bets, and the folder supports doing them in that order.

Agentless, the Agent Skills spec, Aider's repo-then-edit loop, moatless-tools, and the careful slice of Superpowers all land in `agent.yaml`, `prompts/`, and `skills/` without a training run. Agentless is the one to implement first, because the harness already paid for localization: `search_similar_code`, `get_code_neighbors`, and `get_code_subgraph` are the file-and-symbol stage, and `edit_file` is the repair stage. `submit_patch` is the validation gate. Superpowers is the surprising negative result of the README re-read. Its MIT skill library is real, and its spec-then-plan-then-verify order matches BCF. Its test-driven step writes a failing test into the project. Container B throws those edits away and grades the official `test_patch`. The skill to write is "reproduce with the tests that already exist, then edit production code."

The train rows are real repositories. A competition adapter still has to load on `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`. SWE-smith's live README confirms the collection's account: MIT license, 52k instances, 26k trajectories, a Qwen 2.5 Coder LoRA reported at 40.2% pass@1 on SWE-bench Verified, and GRPO via SkyRL. The same README states that environment builds target Ubuntu and are not planned for Windows or macOS. The local Unsloth note is the procedure to follow, with its own warning that the Modal script defaults to an H100, a 10,240-token sequence, and 4-bit loading turned off. PEFT is what the harness knows how to load. TRL is the SFT and GRPO API the Unsloth path sits on. bitsandbytes is the quantization piece for a 31B QLoRA. Axolotl, LlamaFactory, and ms-swift are substitutes for the trainer, not extra data.

mini-swe-agent is the result that should change the training plan. The live README says the agent has no tools other than bash, keeps a linear history, and claims above 74% on SWE-bench Verified. A linear history is a gift for SFT. A bash-only history is the wrong action language for a server whose tool parser is `gemma4`. Convert traces into the nine harness tools before training, or the adapter will learn a dialect the scorer never sees. SWE-agent's own trajectories have the same conversion problem, with a different tool dialect. The digest is explicit that SWE-smith's tutorial still uses SWE-agent for collection.

R2E-Gym, SWE-Gym, SWE-Master, and SWE-Swiss are additional task and recipe sources. They are older or smaller than SWE-smith. swe-rl's rule-based reward is the right objective, and its API license field is `NOASSERTION`, so the idea travels more safely than the code. rLLM, SkyRL, verl, OpenRLHF, prime-rl, verifiers, Dressage, and NeMo Gym are the RL shelf. SkyRL is the one SWE-smith names. None of them is the first week of work.

## How to test, in order

Score every change on the 129 public tasks with the competition harness. That is the only set in this repo that shares the grader, the wheels, the graphs, and the offline install. SWE-bench and `sb-cli` are the external proxy with the most written-down protocol. SWE-bench-Live is the better anti-memorization check, because the digest describes it as continuously rebuilt. SWE-rebench-V2 and multi-swe-bench add more fail-to-pass tasks, the latter across languages the sandbox is not built for. Harbor and Terminal-Bench measure long terminal jobs. They are worth a slice once `run_command` use is the thing being changed. EvalPlus, LiveCodeBench, and BigCodeBench measure generated functions. A gain there can coexist with a loss on repository patches.

vLLM belongs in the test column because a local server that does not match `max_model_len=32768`, `tool_call_parser=gemma4`, and `max_lora_rank=128` will flatter a submission that then fails on the eval machine.

## The remainder of the collection

The inventory is large because the source digests are large. Below the ranked table, the chance of moving this leaderboard drops to about zero.

| Screen | What happened |
| --- | --- |
| Files read | githubdigest.json plus 39 markdown files under foss_tools/grokmuse. Thirteen pairs are byte-identical mirrors, so the folder is smaller than a file count suggests. |
| Unique GitHub repos | 4,248 after de-duplicating owner/repo links. The JSON catalog alone holds 4,095 records. |
| Keyword screen | 686 records matched a broad agent, train, eval, graph, or skill pattern. A tighter pass left 191. |
| Main table | 44 resources scored 56 or above on the weighted rubric. |
| Left off on purpose | The rest of the 4,248. Design skills, voice, SEO, office suites, monitors, games, and generic Claude or Codex wrappers have no path to a pytest-passing Python patch under this harness. |

Code-graph products show up often: `colbymchenry/codegraph` (digest: 63,894 stars, MIT, C; a daily note says 72,447), `Graphify-Labs/graphify` (digest-side figure 99,823; an X listicle the same week says 122,279), `DeusData/codebase-memory-mcp`, `redhat-et/ripwire`, `jgravelle/jcodemunch-mcp`, and `Cranot/roam-code`. They rebuild a graph at runtime and speak MCP. This contest already mounts a NetworkX call graph and 256-dimensional embeddings, and the scorer's container has no network and no extra MCP server. Time spent wrapping a second graph is time not spent deciding which `search_similar_code` query to issue.

Design skills, voice models, SEO crawlers, office runtimes, uptime monitors, and Claude or Codex plugin marketplaces dominate the Instagram and daily X files. `anthropics/skills` is the example repo the Agent Skills README points at. The digest star figure is 165,666 and was not re-counted. The spec repo is the one that changes how skills are written. Example packs for logos, CSS, and Docker do not change a pytest result.

Base code models in the LoRA digest, including DeepSeek-Coder, DeepSeek-Coder-V2, StarCoder2, Seed-Coder, and Code Llama, cannot be the submitted checkpoint. The rules require `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct` for every agent. Qwen appears only as the teacher inside the SWE-smith recipe. Magicoder, OctoPack, SelfCodeAlign, OpenCoder, and WizardLM are instruction-tuning recipes for code completion. That distribution is next-token source code, which is a weak stand-in for an issue, a localization trace, and a minimal diff.

DeepSpeed (43,167 stars in the digest) and Liger-Kernel (6,569 stars, BSD-2-Clause) can speed a training job. They do not choose the data. `microsoft/LoRA` and the QLoRA paper repo are the history of the method. PEFT is the implementation the harness names. Cactus Hybrid and TurboFieldfare, both in `githubdigest.json`, run other Gemma checkpoints, including a cloud handoff and a 26B mixture-of-experts build. The scored weights are the 31B W4A16 QAT model on vLLM.

## Open questions

No source in the collection, and none of the READMEs opened today, shows a working LoRA or QLoRA config for `gemma-4-31b-it-qat-w4a16-ct`. Axolotl's digest synopsis lists Gemma as a family. That sentence was generated into `githubdigest.json`. The live PEFT quickstart uses Qwen. Until a rank-16 adapter saves, loads under vLLM's Gemma 4 parser, and calls `edit_file`, the training half of this table is a plan.

Solve rates were not reproduced. SWE-smith's 40.2% and its "+32% jump" wording, mini-swe-agent's ">74%", SWE-Swiss's 60.2%, and Agentless's December 2024 figures are repository claims. The collection's own caveats say the same thing.

Star counts disagree inside the folder when the source is an X or Instagram listicle. The ranked table prefers the GitHub-API figures from the 30 September LoRA digests. Superpowers' roughly 292,000 stars, the Agent Skills 23,713, Aider's 48,283, and the code-graph stars were not re-read from the GitHub counter today. The Superpowers repository and its MIT license were re-read.

Several high-value repos have no SPDX license in the collection: SWE-agent, SWE-bench, TRL, SkyRL, rLLM, and vLLM among them. swe-rl, the Augment SWE-bench agent, LiteLLM, and Langfuse are recorded as `NOASSERTION`. A winning submission has to ship under an OSI license. Training-only use of an unclear repo needs a look at its LICENSE file before code is copied into the write-up.

SWE-smith does not say whether its 52k instances include the four public contest repositories. Overlap would inflate a local score. The hidden set is private, so public SWE-bench success can still miss the leaderboard. The 12-hour clock is about six minutes per hidden task if the run is serial. Agentless-style multi-sample reranking and a large thinking budget both spend that clock.

R2E-Gym/DeepSWE, WizardCoder as its own repository, `ise-uiuc/RepoCoder`, and `aorwall/moatless-codeagents` are recorded as GitHub 404s in the leftover digest. They are not usable rows.
